# Combinatorial twisted bialgebras and combinatorial twisted double bialgebras 

Loïc Foissy 

Univ. Littoral Côte d’Opale, UR 2597 LMPA, Laboratoire de Mathématiques Pures et Appliquées Joseph Liouville F-62100 Calais, France. Email: foissy@univ-littoral.fr arXiv:2610.06256v1 [math.CO] 5 Oct 2026 Abstract 

We provide in this text a new framework for a family of combinatorial Hopf algebras. We adopt for this the language of species and twisted bialgebras (also known as Hopf monoids). We define the category of combinatorial twisted bialgebras (briefly, CTBs), a class of twisted bialgebras satisfying strong combinatorial conditions of compatibilities of the product and the coproduct with the underlying combinatorial structure. The considered morphisms also have to respect the combinatorial structure, in some sense. Many classical examples fit into this framework, including Hopf algebras of graphs, rooted trees, posets, hypergraphs, symmetric functions, boolean maps, etc. Most of known combinatorial Hopf algebras can be obtained from a CTB by application of a Fock functor, or by duality. A central result is the introduction of a universal CTB denoted by Exch , built from collections of compositions satisfying a certain exchange condition. It has a special character ϵ, such that the pair pExch , ϵ q plays a role analogous to that of quasi-symmetric functions for graded and connected Hopf algebras: it is the terminal object in the category of CTBs equipped with combinatorial characters. This leads to universal morphisms that encode important information about the combinatorial structure of coproducts. The natural order on the set of compositions on a fixed set is used to define three important sub-objects Exch ´, Exch ` and Exch `` of Exch .We also studies polynomial invariants associated with CTBs. It establishes a correspondence between characters and polynomial invariants. Well-known invariants such as the chromatic polynomial of graphs and the Ehrhart polynomials of posets arise naturally within this frame-work. We show that, for a given degree, only finitely many polynomial invariants can occur. We show that these universal polynomial invariants can be used to give cancellation-free formulas for the antipode or the Eulerian idempotent, under conditions on the CTB, and use this for an example based on rooted trees, coming from a Hopf algebra used in linguistics. Results are also obtained for families of polynomial invariants with the alternating sign condition, or satisfying a reciprocity principle. We also extend the theory to combinatorial twisted double bialgebras (briefly, CTDBs), which involve two interacting coproduct structures. These generalize previously known examples arising from graphs, posets, hypergraphs, and rooted trees. The twisted approach preserves the combinatorial richness of these structures while providing a unified algebraic framework. We show that not any CTB can be made a CTDB, leading to the introduction of the notion of quasi-cocommutativity. In particular, we show that Exch ` is not a CTDB, and construct a sub-object which is, in a unique way, and maximal for the inclusion. In the cocommutative case, simplifications naturally occur, and we show that in this case, a CTB of hypergraphs plays a central role, especially two sub-objects based on connectedness systems and antichain covers. We also show that, in the cocommutative case, the universal polynomial invariants are always chromatic polynomials of hypergraphs, no matter which CTB is considered. Finally, forgetting the combinatorial underlying structure, we study the algebraic structure of 

Exch , with the help of a twisted version of Loday and Ronco’s rigidity theorem for infinitesimal bialgebras.

# Combinatorial Hopf algebras 

In the past decades, numerous Hopf algebras or bialgebras based on combinatorial objects appear in the literature. An important example occurred in the nineties with Connes and Kreimer’s work on the Renormalization in Quantum Field Theory [19, 20, 21], where this mathematical object, an inductive procedure of extractions of poles, was described as a Birkhoff decomposition in the group of characters of certain Hopf algebras based on Feynman graphs. A toy-model for these objects is the celebrated Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra of rooted trees, which was quickly related by duality to another Hopf algebra, introduced by Grossman and Larson in a context of differential calculus [46, 44, 45, 70, 52]. Similar Hopf algebras of rooted trees also appeared in Hairer’s theory of regularity structures, introduced to solve stochastics PDEs [47, 14, 15], with a heavy complementary structure on the vertices and on the edges. Other ones also appeared recently in linguistics, in Marcolli, Chomsky and Berwick’s work [64]. To stay with trees, we can also cite other Hopf algebras of planar trees or binary trees, with a similar coproduct of admissible cuts [29, 30, 54, 57], some related to the theory of operads. Other Hopf algebras with a strong combinatorial flavor appear in the theory of representa-tions of family of groups (especially symmetric groups), such as the Hopf algebra of symmetric functions Sym , its generalization the Hopf algebra of quasi-symmetric functions QSym and its dual the Hopf algebra of quasi-symmetric functions NSym [43, 49, 61]. Permutations them-selves can be organized as a Hopf algebra into several ways, some commutative [50], the most interesting being perhaps the one known as the Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebra, also called the Hopf algebra of free quasi-symmetric functions FQSym [22, 61, 31]. In a similar spirit, Hopf-algebraic structures are defined on packed words (or equivalently, set compositions) [68], parking functions [69], uniform block permutations [4], etc. Many results of graph theory have been lifted to the level of Hopf algebras, thanks to bial-gebraic structures on graphs [34, 41]. In particular, the chromatic polynomial can be seen as a Hopf algebra morphism from graphs to polynomials, as well as the Fortuin and Kasteleyn polynomial (a variant of Tutte’s polynomial). These results are extended to hypergraphs, giving several notions of chromatic polynomials or symmetric functions [36, 23, 6], mixed graphs [37] or to posets and quasi-posets [62, 33, 42]. One can also cite noncrossing partitions, organized as a Hopf algebra which plays a major role in the theory of free probability [38, 24, 67]. Despite all these examples and applications, there is no consensual definition of a combina-torial Hopf algebra. A tentative has been given by Aguiar, Bergeron and Sottile [2]. In their setting, a combinatorial Hopf algebra (CHA) is a pair formed by a graded and connected Hopf algebra H and a character f of H. The main interest is that QSym , with a particular character 

ϵ, is a terminal object in the category of CHAs as they define it. This allows to define morphisms from combinatorial objects to quasi-symmetric functions in a purely algebraic way, such as the chromatic polynomial symmetric function of a graph [75]. We won’t follow this definition here, but we will use the formalism of twisted bialgebras (with the terminology of [71]), also called Hopf monoids, developed in [3]. 5Species and twisted objects 

Let us detail what these objects are. The category of species was introduced by Joyal [55, 56], in order to give a better understanding of classical manipulations of formal series in combinatorics. Formally, a species is a functor from the category of finite sets, with bijections, to the category of sets, or of K-vector spaces for linear species. In other words, for a species Q:• For any finite set X, QrXs is a set, or is a K-vector space if Q is a linear species. • For any bijection σ : X ÝÑ Y , Qrσs : QrXs ÝÑ QrY s is a map, linear if Q is a linear species. These correspondence are of course compatible with the composition of bijections, see Definition 1.1.1 for the full details. Here are classical examples of species, which will be used all along this text. • Graphs. For any finite set X, GrXs is the set of graphs whose set of vertices is X. For example,

Linear species form a symmetric monoidal category for the Cauchy tensor product, which allows to define algebras, coalgebras, bialgebras, etc, in the category of species. We now give a short review of these objects, see Definitions 1.1.2, 1.1.3 and 1.1.4 for the full details. In a twisted algebra Q, if X and Y are disjoint finite sets, there exists a map mX,Y : QrXsb QrY s ÝÑ 

QrX\Y s, with an associativity constraint. In a twisted coalgebra Q, if X and Y are disjoint finite sets, there exists a map ∆X,Y : QrX \ Y s ÝÑ QrXs b QrY s, with a coassociativity constraint. A twisted bialgebra is both a twisted algebra and a twisted coalgebra, with a compatibility between the product and the coproduct. Here are some examples: • Graphs. The linearization of G is a twisted bialgebra. The product of two graphs with disjoint sets of vertices is their disjoint union. For example,

Roughly speaking, this functor consists to "forget" the elements of the underlying sets, and keeping only the structure of the elements of Q. For example: • Graphs. Mostly all combinatorial Hopf algebras (whatever we mean by this) can be applied by the applica-tion of a Fock functor to a twisted bialgebra, see [3] for more examples. We mention here also the noncommutative Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra [54, 29, 30] and its dual, the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra [46, 44], the Hopf algebra of free quasi-symmetric functions [61, 22], the Hopf algebra of noncrossing partitions used in the theory of free probability [67, 24], and various bialgebras on hypergraphs [23, 6, 36]. 

# Combinatorial twisted bialgebras 

We focus in this text on Hopf algebras coming from a twisted bialgebra by the application of a Fock functor (mostly, the bosonic functor F), assuming that this twisted bialgebra ℓQ is the linearization of a species Q (that is to say, for any finite set X, QrXs is a basis of ℓQrXs), with the following properties: • QrHs is reduced to the unit of ℓQ.• The product mX,Y pa b bq of two elements a P QrXs and b P QrY s belongs to QrX \ Y s,or is zero. • The coproduct ∆X,Y pbq of an element of QrX \ Y s is a tensor of an element of QrXs and an element of QrY s, or is zero. 9This is what we shall call a combinatorial twisted bialgebra (briefly, CTB), see Definition 2.1.1. This includes the Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra and its noncommutative version, as well as its decorated ones, the Hopf algebras of hypergraphs, of posets, of quasi-posets, of noncross-ing partitions, the Hopf algebra Sym of symmetric functions, and many more. This does not include the Hopf algebra of quasi-symmetric functions QSym , or its generalizations on uniform block partitions, packed words or parking functions, or the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebras, but includes their duals. However, all the known CHAs do not fit here anyway: one may think to the Hopf algebra of quasi-symmetric functions FQSym , or its dual (as it is self-dual), at least with its usual basis of permutations; a change of basis may insert it in this setting, but not in a trivial way. In the sequel, with an abuse of notation, we shall denote the CTB by Q (which is a species) instead of ℓQ (which is a linear species). We organize CTBs into a category: if Q and Q1 are two CTBs, a morphism of CTBs from Q to Q1 is a morphism of twisted bialgebras, sending any element of Q to an element of Q1, or to 0 (Definition 2.1.2). We also define a notion of quotients (Definition 2.1.6). However, if any morphism ϕ : Q ÝÑ Q1 of CTBs induces a morphism 

ϕ : Q{Ker pϕq ÝÑ Im pϕq, this morphism ϕ has no reason to be injective, to the difference with Noether’s theorem. Nevertheless, it does not take the value 0 on Q{Ker pϕq.The strong combinatorial conditions imposed to a CTB B makes its algebraic structure rather rigid. We introduce indecomposable elements (Definition 2.2.1), namely elements of B which cannot be written as a product of two elements of B different from the unit. An easy induction shows that any element of B can be decomposed into a product of indecomposable elements, and the existence of the coproduct imposes that this decomposition is unique, up to the order of the factors (Theorem 2.2.4). In other words, as a twisted algebra, B is generated by the subspecies of indecomposable elements, with certain commutation relations between these elements. For graphs, hypergraphs, posets, rooted trees, and so on, this notion of indecomposability coincide with the notion of connectedness. For other cases (the example Exch to come), it can be more subtle to describe. This decomposition implies that to any element b P BrXs, we can associate an equivalence „pic q 

> b

on X, whose classes are the indecomposable factors of the decomposition of b into indecomposable elements. The number of classes of „pic q 

> b

is denoted by ic pbq. This decomposition will play an important role in the sequel. If X is a finite set, then for any 

b P BrXs, obviously, ic pbq ď | X|. Elements such that ic pbq “ | X| are called the discrete elements of B, and they form a sub-CTB of B, denoted by DB (Definition 2.2.7 and Proposition 2.2.8). Discrete graphs are the graphs without edges, also called totally disconnected. Discrete posets are posets whose order is the equality of the underlying set.

Before the description of the next results of this text, let us give some details on the notion of polynomial invariants. The polynomial algebra KrT s is a bialgebra, with the multiplicative coproduct ∆ defined by 

∆pT q “ T b 1 ` 1 b T. 

If pB, m, ∆q is a bialgebra, a polynomial invariant is a bialgebra map from B to KrXs. Here are examples of polynomial invariants. • Graphs. Let us take B “ FrGs, the bialgebra of graphs of [34, 41]. A first polynomial invariant is given by 

ϕ``pGq “ T |V pGq| ,

where G is a graph and V pGq the set of its vertices. A more interesting one is given by the chromatic polynomial. Recall that if G is a graph, a valid N -coloration of G is a map c : V pGq ÝÑ t 1, . . . , N u such that if two vertices x, y of G are related by an edge, then cpxq ‰ cpyq. There exists a unique polynomial ϕchr pGq such that for any N ě 1,

ϕchr pGqp N q is the number of valid N -colorations of G. Then ϕchr is a polynomial invariant of FrGs. Generalizations on hypergraphs can be found in [23, 6, 36]. • Posets. If P is a poset, we denote by V pP q the set of its vertices, and we denote by nP the number of linear extensions of P , that is to say the number of total orders on V pP q that contains the partial order of P . A first polynomial invariant is given by

# Twisted double bialgebras 

Before introducing combinatorial twisted double bialgebras, let us give some reminders on clas-sical double bialgebras. Let pB, m, δ q be a bialgebra. The category of right comodules is a symmetric monoidal category: if pM, ρ q and pM 1, ρ 1q are two right comodules over B, then 

M b M 1 is also a right comodule over B, with the coaction defined by 

m1,3,24 ˝ p ρ b ρ1q,

where 

m1,3,24 :

" M b B b M 1 b B ÝÑ M b M 1 b Bx b a b x1 b b Þ ÝÑ x b x1 b ab. 

Therefore, it is possible to define bialgebras in the category of right comodules over B, and this is precisely what a pair of cointeracting bialgebras is. More precisely, it is given by two bialgebras 

pA, m, ∆q and pB, m, δ q, and a map ρ : A ÝÑ A b B, such that: • ρ is a right coaction of B over A.• The product m of A is a right comodule morphism: 

ρ ˝ m “ m12 ,34 ˝ p ρ b ρq.

The unit 1A of A is a right comodule morphism, that is to say ρp1Aq “ 1A b 1B . This item is equivalent to the fact that ρ is an algebra morphism from A to A b B.• The coproduct ∆ of A is a right comodule morphism: 

p∆ b Id B q ˝ ρ “ m1,3,24 ˝ p ρ b ρq ˝ ∆.

• The counit ε∆ of A is a right comodule morphism: 

@a P A, pε∆ b Id B q ˝ ρpaq “ ε∆paq1B .

A double bialgebra is a pair of cointeracting bialgebras pA, m, ∆q, and pB, m, δ q, with the coaction 

ρ, such that pA, m q “ p B, m q, and ρ “ δ. Except from the base field K, the simplest case of double bialgebra is the polynomial algebra KrT s, with its two multiplicative coproducts defined by 

∆pT q “ T b 1 ` 1 b T, δpT q “ T b T. 

Various combinatorial examples are known. It seems that the first one is due to Calaque, Ebrahimi-Fard and Manchon [17], where a second coproduct δ on the Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra with the help of contraction of edges, making it double. Other examples can be 15 found on graphs [34], oriented graphs [63], hypergraphs [23, 36], quasi-posets or finite topologies [26, 33], noncrossing partitions [25, 38], etc. Let us now give a review on theoretical results on double bialgebras, before turning to their twisted version. Most of these results come from [35]. Let pB, m, ∆, δ q be a double bialgebra. The set Char pBq of characters of B inherits two products ‹ and ˚, dual respectively to ∆ and δ.The cointeraction implies that for any f , g, h P Char pBq,

Let Q be a double bialgebra. We show in Theorem 1.3.6 that there exists a unique polynomial invariant of Q, compatible with the coproduct δ. It will be called the fundamental polynomial invariant. For graphs, this is the chromatic polynomial, and for posets the strict Ehrhart poly-nomial. Again, the action of the monoid of characters of Q (dual to the coproduct δq acts freely on the set of polynomial invariants of B, with as a single generator the universal polynomial invariant (Proposition 1.3.7). As in the classical setting, the antipode of pQ, m, ∆q is obtained from the universal polynomial invariant and δ, through an evaluation in ´1 (Theorem 1.3.10). Let us now turn to our combinatorial frame. A double structure on a CTB B is a coproduct 

δ making a twisted double bialgebra, with the combinatorial extra condition that for any finite set X, for any b P BrXs, and any „P ErXs, δ„pbq is a tensor of an element of BrX{„s with an element of BrXs, or is zero (Definition 4.1.1). Such an object will be called a combinatorial twisted double bialgebra (briefly, CTDB). For example, G and P are CTDBs. An important question is to know if a given CTB can be made into a CTDB by the addition of a coproduct δ,and if such a δ is unique. We give partial answers to these questions. Firstly, if B is a CTDB, we associate to any b P B a set of equivalences Erbs, whose elements are the equivalences „ such that δ„pbq ‰ 0. A particular class of CTDBs is given by the ones for which CB “ DB: these are called regular, and we give in Proposition 4.1.15 several characterizations of regularity. The CTDB of graphs G and of posets P are regular, and more generally, if pB, m, ∆, δ q is a CTDB such that pB, m, ∆q is non-degenerate, then B is regular (Proposition 4.1.16). The irregularity of a CTDB has to be understood as a defect of graduation, in some sense, and we explain how to reduce ourselves to the regular case. For a regular CTDB B, the fundamental polynomial invariant is ϕB

> `

# Quasi-cocommutativity 

In order to answer the question if a given CTB can be made double, we introduce the tech-nical notion of quasi-cocommutativity (Definition 4.2.1). In rough words, a CTB B is quasi-cocommutative if it is commutative, and if the coproduct is given by a restriction operation on the underlying combinatorial objects, at least when the factors in the coproduct are indecom-posable. We prove that non-degenerate CTDBs are quasi-cocommutative, which implies that some CTBs cannot be made double. This is the case for Exch , Exch ` and Exch ´, and also for one of the CTB on hypergraphs, whose fundamental polynomial invariant is the chromatic 17 polynomial of [6], see Example 4.2.3. The question is now to determine if a quasi-cocommutative CTB can be made double. We firstly study, for a quasi-cocommutative CTB B, the structure of the equivalences associated to any element b of B in the case it is made double. We show that these sets Erbs are entirely defined by the decomposition into indecomposable elements and the coproduct ∆ (Definition 4.2.5), as well as the right factor b |„ of δ„pbq if „P Erbs. There is a little bit more latitude for the left factor b{„ , but not so much, as C`pb, „q “ C`pb{„q is also entirely determined by the decomposition into indecomposable elements and ∆ (Definition 4.2.6). These characterizations are enough to construct a coaction-like map Ξ : B ÝÑ Exch ` bB

(Theorem 4.2.10) for any quasi-cocommutative CTB, which is pΓB 

> `

b Id Bq ˝ δ if δ makes B double. It turns out this map Ξ is enough to generalize the formula for the antipode for CTDBs to quasi-cocommutative CTBs (Corollary 4.2.13), and to show that the polynomial invariant ϕB

Of course, there exist non quasi-cocommutative CTBs, but we show that any commutative CTB B contains a quasi-cocommutative sub-CTB Bqcom , maximal for the inclusion (Theorem 4.2.18), and that this construction is functorial in the category of commutative CTBs (Proposition 4.2.19). This coproduct ∆L conserves the number of leaves and the number of vertices. It induces a quotient to reduced rooted trees, that is to say to trees such that no vertex has exactly one child:

This coproduct ∆ℓ conserves the number of leaves, but not of vertices. 18 The coproduct ∆L can be lifted to the CTB level into two ways, each one with its qualities and its defects. The first one is based on vertices: for any finite set X, we consider the set of rooted forests RF rXs whose set of vertices is X, and this defines a species RF (Definition 6.1.1). The coproduct ∆L is lifted to RF , making it a quasi-cocommutative CTB (Theorem 6.1.3). This allows us to describe easily the antipode of RF in Corollary 6.1.7. However, because ∆ℓ

does not conserve the number of vertices, it is not possible to obtain a twisted version of ∆ℓ

from it, unfortunately. For this, we introduce a second CTB, based on leaves: for any finite set X, RF ℓrXs is the set of isoclasses of rooted forests whose set of leaves is X (Definition 6.2.1). This defines a CTB RF ℓ (Theorem 6.2.2). However, it is not quasi-cocommutative. The advantage of this construction is the existence of a morphism of CTBs from RF ℓ to one of non quasi-cocommutative CTB of hypergraphs (Theorem 6.2.4). This morphism is non injective, and induces consequently a quotient of RF ℓ embedded in hypergraphs: this quotient is the species of reduced rooted forests RRF ℓ, and the induced coproduct is a CTB version of ∆ℓ

(Theorem 6.2.9). We show that the Fock functor applied to RF and to RF ℓ both gives the same object, precisely the bialgebra of rooted trees with the coproduct ∆L (Theorem 6.3.1), and the quasi-cocommutativity of ∆L gives a cancellation-free formula for its antipode. Combining both constructions, we obtain a cancellation-free formula for the (more complicated) antipode of ∆ℓ

on reduced forests (Proposition 6.3.4), with a combinatorial interpretation of its coefficients in terms of partitions in trees, which answers a question in [78]. 

# Structures related to the Hadamard product 

The category of species has a second monoidal structure, the Hadamard product b: if P and Q

are two species, and X is a finite set, 

P b QrXs “ PrXs b QrXs.

We explore in the fifth chapter some applications of this tensor product. A first, immediate application is that the Hadamard product of two CTBs is also a CTB (Proposition 5.1.2). This allows to construct new CTBs from old ones: for example, the Hopf algebra of mixed graphs of [37] can be obtained by the Hadamard product of the CTB of graphs and the CTB of oriented graphs. Unfortunately, this does not work for CTDBs. We define an internal product on a CTB B as an associative map from B bB to B, compatible with the coproduct ∆ and the product m of B (Definition 5.1.4). For example, the intersection is an internal product on Exch ´ and Exch ` (Proposition 5.1.5); The union of sets of edges or hyperedges is an internal product on graphs or hypergraphs (Proposition 5.1.5); the transitive closure of the union is an internal product on quasi-posets (Proposition 5.1.8). It is noticeable that none of these internal products subsist at the application of the bosonic Fock functor. Nevertheless, they are compatible with the full Fock functor. Another application, probably less anecdotal than the internal product, is given by functors of colorations. If Ω is a set, we define a CTB DΩ: for any finite set X, DΩrXs is the set of maps from X to Ω, what we will call Ω-colorations. Its product is given by disjoint union, and the coproduct by restriction. For any CTB B, B b DΩ is the Ω-colored version of B, and 

FΩrBs “ FrB b DΩs has for basis the set of isoclasses of Ω-colored elements of B (Proposition 5.2.2). For example, when applied to the CTB of rooted trees, we obtain the colored versions of the Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra [19], where vertices are decorated by elements of the set Ω. When B is a CTDB and Ω is a commutative semigroup, we prove that FΩrBs is also a CTDB (Proposition 5.2.5). This is used to define the formal flow associated to a CTB B,following what is done for Novikov algebras in [7]. A (finite) totally ordered set Ω “ t ω1, . . . , ω nu

being fixed, consider in FΩrBs a family of infinitesimal characters pιωqωPΩ indexed by Ω, which acts only on elements of degree 1, according to their coloration. Then consider in the group of characters of FΩrBs, the element 

exp pιω1 q ˚ ¨ ¨ ¨ ˚ exp pιωn q.

fΩ “ ln pexp pιω1 q ˚ ¨ ¨ ¨ ˚ exp pιωn qq .

As we see, this is closely related to the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula in the exponentiation of the Lie algebra of infinitesimal characters of FΩrBs. The polynomial invariant associated to the character exp pιω1 q ˚ ¨ ¨ ¨ ˚ exp pιωn q is the formal flow ϕfΩ of FΩrBs (Definition 5.2.7), and f

# Functorial sub-objects of a CTB B

We introduce, for a given CTB B, several sub-CTBs, correcting a defect of B, each of these constructions being functorial. The first one is cocom B, the greatest cocommutative sub-CTB (Proposition 5.3.2). In the case of a non-degenerate CTDB, cocom B is a sub-CTDB. We give a description of cocom Exch in terms of invariance under the action of symmetric groups (Definition 5.3.4), which leads us to a description in terms of subsets of equivalences. A more convenient description of cocom Exch ` is lead in Chapter 7, through a family of hypergraphs called antichain covers. The second one is linked with the reciprocity principle of posets. As a well-known property of Ehrhart polynomials (which we prove here in Corollary 5.3.9 in an algebraic way), for any poset P ,

A classical result on graphs is the property of alternating sign of their chromatic polynomial: if G is a graph with n vertices, we write its chromatic polynomial as

We shall say that G satisfies the alternating sign condition (Definition 5.3.12). We show that any CTB B has a sub-CTB Balt , maximal for the inclusion, satisfying the alternating sign condition (Proposition 5.3.13). We also show that the other fundamental polynomial invariants will have at least a zero coefficient between two non-zero coefficients.

# Bialgebraic structure of Exch 

The last chapter is devoted to the study of Exch and its sub-objects Exch ˘ and Exch ``,as twisted bialgebras. We show that all of them are, in a non-trivial way, isomorphic to shuffle twisted bialgebras. To prove this, we use a twisted version of Loday and Ronco’s rigidity theorem for cofree bialgebras [58]. We firstly introduce twisted infinitesimal bialgebras (Definition 8.1.3), and prove a rigidity theorem for them (Theorem 8.1.5),with the help of the antipode. We also prove Theorem 8.1.9, which establishes that any commutative cofree twisted bialgebra is isomorphic to a shuffle algebra, thanks to the eulerian idempotent. These results are used on 

Exch and its three sub-objects Exch `, Exch ´ and Exch ``, thanks to a new concatenation-like product ˛ on Exch , Exch ` and Exch `` (Theorem 8.1.11), and to another product ˛ on Exch ´

(Theorem 8.1.14). 22 Notations

23 Chapter 1 

# Reminders and notations 

# Introduction 

This first chapter is devoted to reminders and complements on the objects which will be used in the sequel: species, twisted algebras, coalgebras, bialgebras, Hopf algebras, etc. We start with species. These objects were introduced by Joyal [55, 56] in order to have a better understanding of the behavior of generating formal series associated to a family of combinatorial objects. For example, graphs, hypergraphs, posets, partitions, equivalences, compositions, and many more, are organized as species. Species are functors from the category of finite sets with bijections, to the category of sets with maps (set species) or to the category of vector spaces over K with linear maps (linear species). The category of linear species is a symmetric tensor category with the Cauchy tensor product, and this allows to define algebras, coalgebras, bialgebras, etc, in this category: these are what we call twisted algebras, coalgebras, bialgebras, etc [71]. We give the definitions in the first section of this chapter, as well as examples which will play an important role in the sequel: the species of equivalences E and the species of compositions Comp ,see Example 1.1.1. We generalize well-known results from classical objects to twisted ones in the second section. We firstly introduce the dual Q˚ of a species Q (Definition 1.2.1). When Q is a twisted coalgebra, then its dual is a (classical) algebra with the convolution product (Proposition 1.2.2). The natural graduation of a species induces a metric on Q˚, making it a complete metric space (Propositions 1.2.3, and 1.2.4). This allows to define the action of the algebra Krr T ss of formal series on Q˚,and more generally on the space of species morphisms between two species (Theorem 1.2.6). This allows for example to determine the inverse for the convolution product of an element of Q˚

(Proposition 4.2.12), and to define the exponential and the logarithm of certain elements of Q˚

(Proposition 1.2.8). We then define characters and infinitesimal characters of a twisted bialgebra 

Q (Definition 1.2.9), generalizing the equivalent notions for bialgebras. When Q is connected, that is to say if QrHs is 1-dimensional, the exponential and the logarithm define bijections between infinitesimal characters and characters, inverse one from the other (Corollary 1.2.12), as for classical connected bialgebras. We also give the definition of twisted Hopf algebras and give the twisted version of Takeuchi’s formula [76] (Definition 1.2.13 and Proposition 1.2.14). We introduce the twisted version of the eulerian idempotent [8, 16, 35] in Definition 1.2.15. If Q is a commutative or cocommutative twisted bialgebra, then the eulerian idempotent is a projection (which justifies its name), on the space of primitive elements when Q is cocommutative, vanishing on the square of the augmentation ideal when Q is commutative (Theorem 1.2.19). The proof use some combinatorial interpretations in term of packed matrices. We then introduce polynomial invariants of a twisted bialgebra Q (Definition 1.2.20). They correspond, at the classical level, to bialgebra morphisms taking their values in the polynomial bialgebra pKrT s, m, ∆q, where m is the usual product of polynomials and ∆ the coproduct defined by 

∆pT q “ T b 1 ` 1 b T. 

24 We show that these polynomial invariants are in a bijective correspondence with characters (Theorem 1.2.23). The character associated to a polynomial invariant is given by the evaluation at T “ 1, and we give an explicit formula for the converse bijection, with the help of Hilbert polynomials. Using the exponential-logarithm bijection, we can also associate bijectively a poly-nomial invariant to any infinitesimal character (Proposition 1.2.25). Here, Hilbert polynomials are replaced by monomials T n

n! .In the next section, we introduce the twisted version of double bialgebras. A twisted dou-ble bialgebra is a twisted bialgebra Q with complementary coproducts δ„, associated to any equivalence of any finite set, satisfying good compatibilities (Definition 1.3.1). We show that the characters of a twisted double bialgebra inherit a second convolution product, right-distributive on the first one (Proposition 1.3.2). Among all the polynomial invariants of a twisted double bialgebra Q, only one is compatible with the coproduct δ and its associated counit, KrT s being given the second coproduct δ defined by 

δpT q “ T b T, 

see Definition 1.3.4 and Theorem 1.3.6. It will be called the fundamental polynomial invariant of Q. We show in Proposition 1.3.7 that the monoid of characters of Q, with the product associated to δ, acts (on the right) on the set of polynomial invariants of Q and that this action is freely generated by the fundamental polynomial invariant: in other words, the fundamental polynomial invariant allows to bijectively find all the other polynomial invariants by the action of characters. We also show that the action of formal series of a connected double twisted bialgebra can be simplified thanks to the counit ϵδ of the coproduct δ (Proposition 1.3.9). Consequently, cancellation-free formulas for the antipode or for the eulerian idempotent of a connected twisted double bialgebra can be given, with the help of the fundamental polynomial invariant (Theorem 1.3.10). Reminders on the bosonic Fock functor are given in the last section of this chapter. This functor, defined in [3], associates to any linear species a graded vector space. As it is a functor of symmetric categories, it sends any twisted algebra, coalgebra or bialgebra Q to a classical algebra, coalgebra, or bialgebra FrQs. We show that when Q is a double twisted double bialgebra, then 

FrQs inherits a second coproduct δ making it a double bialgebra, that is to say that pFrQs, m, ∆q

is a bialgebra in the category of right comodules over the bialgebra pFrQs, m, δ q, the coaction being δ itself. The last section of this chapter is about the twisted quasi-shuffle algebra of compositions 

Comp . We give the twisted version of Aguiar, Bergeron and Sottile’s theorem on the terminal property of QSym [2]: we first introduce the quasi-shuffle product ] on Comp (Theorem 1.5.5), thanks to a cofreeness property (Proposition 1.5.4). Then the twisted bialgebra pℓComp , ], ∆q,together with a character ϵ, satisfies a terminal property (Theorem 1.5.6). it is noticeable that the application of the Fock functor sends ℓComp to QSym .

Similarly, a K-linear species (shortly, linear species) is a functor from the category of finite sets, with bijections, to the category of K-vector spaces, with linear maps. Morphism of linear species are natural transformations ϕ, such that for any finite set X, ϕX is linear. There exists a functor ℓ from the category of species to the category of K-linear species, sending a species Q

to the linear species ℓQ, defined as follows: • For any finite set X, ℓQrXs is the vector space generated by the set QrXs. In other words, 

QrXs is a basis of ℓQrXs.• For any bijection σ : X ÝÑ Y between two finite sets, ℓQrσs : ℓQrXs ÝÑ ℓQrY s is the unique linear map such that ℓQrσs|QrXs “ Qrσs.

Remark 1.1.1 . If Q and R are two twisted algebras, respectively coalgebras, then Q b R is also a twisted algebra, respectively coalgebra. In a twisted bialgebra Q, the product is a morphism of twisted coalgebras from Q b Q to Q, whereas the coproduct is a morphism of twisted algebras from Q to Q b Q.

We shall say that f is a character of Q if f p1Qq “ 1 and for any pair pX, Y q of disjoint finite sets, any a P QrXs, b P QrY s, f pab q “ f paqf pbq. The set of characters of Q is denoted by Char pQq.2. We shall say that f is an infinitesimal character of Q if for any pair pX, Y q of disjoint finite sets, any a P QrXs, b P QrY s, f pab q “ ε∆paqf pbq ` f paqε∆pbq. The subspace of infinitesimal character of Q is denoted by InfChar pQq.Remark 1.2.2 . Let f P InfChar pQq.1. f p1Qq “ f p1Q1Qq “ ε∆p1Qqf p1Qq ` f p1Qqε∆p1Qq “ 2f p1Qq, so f p1Qq “ 0.2. If pX, Y q is a pair of disjoint nonempty finite sets, a P QrXs, and b P QrY s, then f pab q “ 0.

Example 1.2.2 . With the identification of Example 1.2.1, a character of ℓCom corresponds to a formal series exp pλT q, with λ P K. An infinitesimal character of ℓCom corresponds to a formal series λT , with λ P K.

1.2.2 Twisted Hopf algebras and antipodes 

Definition 1.2.13. Let Q be a twisted bialgebra. We shall say that it is a twisted Hopf algebra if Id Q has an inverse for the convolution product of Hom pQ, Qq. If so, its inverse is called the antipode of Q and denoted by SQ. In other terms, for any finite set X, and any x P QrXs,

1.4.2 Bosonic Fock functor of a twisted double bialgebra 

Let pQ, m, ∆, δ q be a twisted double bialgebra. We define a second coproduct Frδs on FrQs as follows: if X is a finite set,

δpxq.

pFrQs, m, ∆, δ q is a double bialgebra, that is to say: 53 1. pFrQs, m, ∆q is a bialgebra. 2. pFrQs, m, δ q is a bialgebra. 3. ∆ : FrQs ÝÑ FrQs b FrQs is a right comodule morphism: 

`∆ b Id FrQs

Example 1.4.4 . Let us consider the second coproduct δ on KrT s induced by the twisted double structure of B, with the notations of Example 1.4.1. We obtain that δ is the multiplicative product given by 

δpT q “ T b T. 

It is a very classical example of double bialgebra. 

Remark 1.4.1 . Let Q be a twisted bialgebra. We saw that a polynomial invariant is a morphism of bialgebras from pFrQs, m, ∆q to pKrT s, m, ∆q. If Q is a double twisted bialgebra, then a double polynomial invariant is a morphism of double bialgebras from pFrQs, m, ∆, δ q to pKrT s, m, ∆, δ q,and a polynomial invariant compatible with δ is a morphism of bialgebras from pFrQs, m, ∆q to 

pKrT s, m, ∆q, which is also a morphism of non counitary coalgebras from pFrQs, δ q to pKrT s, δ q.

As f is a character, ϵ ˝ Φ “ ϵ ˝ Ψ. By Proposition 1.5.4 (unicity), Φ “ Ψ, so ϕf is a morphism of twisted bialgebras from Q to ℓComp .

Remark 1.5.3 . Applying the Fock functor, we obtain the bialgebra FrComp s “ QSym of quasi-symmetric functions, which the quasi-shuffle product and the deconcatenation coproduct, with a character ϵ. It satisfies also a terminal property, in the category of graded and connected bialgebras [2]. 57 Chapter 2 

# The category of combinatorial twisted bialgebras 

# Introduction 

We now introduce combinatorial twisted bialgebras (shortly, CTBs). They are twisted bialgebras obtained by linearization of a set species B, with the following constraints: • For any pair pX, Y q of disjoint finite sets, for any a P BrXs, and b P BrY s, the product of 

ab belongs to BrX \ Y s, or is zero. • For any pair pX, Y q of disjoint finite sets, for any b P BrX \ Y s, the coproduct ∆X,Y pbq is the tensor of an element of BrXs and an element of BrY s, or is zero. See Definition 2.1.1 for the full details. We introduce the main examples which will be used in this text: the CTBs of graphs (Example 2.1.2), of oriented graphs (Example 2.1.3), of hypergraphs (Example 2.1.4, with four different coproducts), of posets and of quasi-posets (Examples 2.1.5 and 2.1.8). CTBs form a category, with the notion of morphism introduced in Definition 2.1.2: a morphism between two CTBs B and B1 is a morphism of twisted bialgebras, sending any element of B to an element of B1 or to 0. We give several examples of morphisms, from oriented graphs to quasi-posets (Example 2.1.13), from quasi-posets to posets (Example 2.1.14), from hypergraphs to boolean functions (Example 2.1.15). We also introduce the notion of biideal of a CTB (Definition 2.1.6), which allows to define quotients and to associate a kernel to any morphism of CTBs, see Proposition 2.1.8. We study the algebraic structure of a CTB in the second section. We introduce the notion of indecomposable elements (Definition 2.2.1), which will play a crucial role in the sequel, and show that any element of a CTB B can be written as a product of indecomposable elements, uniquely up to the order of the factors (Theorem 2.2.4). For graphs, posets, quasi-posets, this corresponds to the well-known decomposition into connected components. This also shows that, as a twisted algebra, any CTB is generated by its indecomposable elements, with commutation relations between some of them. If X is a nonempty finite set and b P BrXs, then 1 ď ic pbq ď | X|:elements for which ic is maximal are called discrete (Definition 2.2.7), and discrete elements form a sub-CTB DB of B, generated in degree 1. This sub-CTB will have an important role for double CTBs, where it will be related to the counit for the second product in most cases, as we shall see in Chapter 4.

2.1.3 Quotients 

Definition 2.1.6. Let pB, m, ∆q be a CTB, and I be a subspecies of B. We shall say that I is a biideal of B if: 1. IrHs “ H .2. For any pair pX, Y q of disjoint sets, any a P BrXs, b P BrY s, if a P IrXs or b P IrY s,then ab P IrX \ Y s.3. For any pair pX, Y q of disjoint sets, and b P IrX \ Y s such that ∆X,Y pbq “ b|LX b b|RY ,with b|LX P BrXs and b|RY P BrY s, then b|LX P IrXs or b|RY P IrY s.

Proposition 2.1.7. Let pB, m, ∆q be a CTB, and I be a biideal of B. Then BzI naturally inherits a CTB structure. Proof. By definition, ℓI is a biideal of the twisted bialgebra ℓB. The quotient ℓB{ℓI is naturally a twisted bialgebra. For any finite set X, the classes of the elements of BrXsz IrXs gives a basis of ℓB{ℓIrXs. Identifying these elements with their classes, this gives BzI a structure of CTB. 

Example 2.1.16 . The subspecies I “ QP zP is a biideal of QP :1. IrHs “ H .2. If P or Q are quasi-posets, and P or Q is not a poset, then P Q is not a poset. 3. Let P P QrX \ Y sz PrX \ Y s, such that ∆X,Y pP q ‰ 0. Therefore, X is a lower part of P .As P is not a poset, there exists x ‰ y P X \ Y , with x ďP y and y ďP x. As X is a lower part of P , both x, y P X, or both x, y P Y . Consequently, either P|X is not a poset, or P|Y

is not a poset. The quotient QP zI is P, as a CTB. 

Proposition 2.1.8. Let B and B1 be CTBs, and ϕ : B ÝÑ B1 be a morphism of CTBs. For any finite set X, we put 

IrXs “ t b P BrXs | ϕpxq “ 0u.

Then I is a biideal of B. Moreover, ϕ induces a morphism CTBs ϕ : BzI ÝÑ B1, and for any finite set X, any b P BzIrXs, ϕpbq ‰ 0.Proof. As ϕp1Bq “ 1B1 ‰ 0, 1B R IrHs , so IrHs “ H . Let pX, Y q be pair pX, Y q of disjoint sets. Let a P IrXs and b P BrXs. Then ϕpab q “ ϕpaqϕpbq “ 0, so ab P IrX \ Y s. Similarly, if 

a P BrXs and b P IrXs, ab P IrX \ Y s. Let b P BrX \ Y s, such that ∆X,Y pbq “ b|LX b b|RY , with 

b|LX P BrXs and b|RY P BrY s. We assume that both ϕpb|LX q and ϕpb|RY q are nonzero. Then 

∆X,Y pϕpbqq “ p ϕ b ϕq ˝ ∆X,Y pbq “ ϕpb|LX q b ϕpb|RY q ‰ 0,

65 so ϕpbq ‰ 0. Dually, if ϕpbq “ 0, then either ϕpb|LX q “ 0 or ϕpb|RY q “ 0, so I is a biideal. By definition, ℓI is included in the kernel of the linearization ℓϕ of ϕ, so ℓϕ induces a morphism of twisted bialgebras ℓϕ : ℓB{ℓI ÝÑ ℓB1. This morphism is precisely the linearization of a morphism ϕ : BzI ÝÑ B1 of CTBs. By construction, if b P BzIrXs, ϕpbq “ ϕpbq ‰ 0.

Remark 2.1.2 . The morphism ϕ is not necessarily injective, as shown in the next example. 

Example 2.1.17 . We consider the morphism ϕ “ ϕQP ˝ ϕOG : OG ÝÑ P of Example 2.1.14. For any finite set X,

Definition 2.2.1. Let pB, m, ∆q be a CTB, X be a nonempty finite set, and b P BrXs. We shall say that b is indecomposable if, for any disjoint finite sets X1, X 2 such that X “ X1 \ X2, for any b1 P BrX1s and b2 P BrX2s,

b “ b1b2 ùñ X1 “ H or X2 “ H .

Example 2.2.1 . 1. For Com (Example 2.1.1), the indecomposable elements are the sets of cardinality one. 2. We consider the CTB G of graphs (Example 2.1.2). A graph G P GrXs is connected if for any composition pX1, X 2q P Comp rXs, there exists x1 P X1, x2 P X2, such that 

tx1, x 2u P EpGq. Then G is indecomposable in pG, m, ∆q if, and only if, G is connected. In the sequel, we shall indifferently use the words indecomposable or connected for these objects.

A similar result is also proved for Exch ``, with WQSym replaced by FQSym ,the Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebra of permutations [61, 22], see Proposition 3.3.5.

# 3.2 Terminal property of Exch 

In this section, we fix a CTB B.

3.2.1 Characters and special twisted subalgebras 

The definition of the character of a twisted bialgebra is given in Definition 1.2.9. In the case of a CTB B, we shall write " f is a character of B" for " f is a character of ℓB". 

Definition 3.2.1. Let B be a CTB, and f be a character of B. We shall say that B is a combinatorial character of B if for any finite set X, and any b P BrXs, f pbq P t 0, 1u.Remark 3.2.1 . In other words, a combinatorial character of B is a morphism of twisted algebras from B to Com .This notion of combinatorial character is related to particular twisted subalgebras: 

Definition 3.2.2. Let B be a CTB and A be a subspecies of B. We shall say that A is a special twisted subalgebra of B if: 

• 1B P ArHs .

• For any pair pX, Y q of disjoint finite sets, for any a P BrXs, b P BrY s,

ab P ArX \ Y s ðñ a P ArXs and b P ArY s.

Proposition 3.2.3. Let B be a CTB and A be a sub-CTB of B. Then A is a special twisted subalgebra of B.Proof. Let X, Y be disjoint finite sets, a P BrXs and b P BrY s. As A is a twisted subalgebra of 

B, if a P ArXs and b P ArY s, then ab P ArX \ Y s. Conversely, as A is a twisted subcoalgebra, by Lemma 2.1.3, ∆X,Y pab q “ a b b P ArXs b ArY s, so a P ArXs and b P ArY s.

Definition 3.2.4. Let B be a CTB. A convenient subspecies of B is a subspecies C of the sub-species of indecomposable elements of B.

There is a bijective correspondence between combinatorial characters, special twisted subal-gebras, and convenient subspecies:

Definition 3.2.14. Let B be a CTB. We shall say that B is non-degenerate if for any finite set

Example 3.2.4 . 1. The CTB of quasi-posets QP is degenerate: if X is a finite set, and P P

C`pGq ‰ H if, and only if, G is acyclic. Therefore, nd OG is the CTB of acyclic oriented graphs OG ac .

Example 3.2.5 . The CTBs Exch and Exch ´ are degenerate: consider for example I “ tp Xqu P

# Double-bialgebraic aspects 

We now introduce combinatorial twisted double bialgebras (briefly, CTDBs), that are both CTBs and twisted double bialgebras, following the definition of the first chapter, with a combinatorial compatibility detailed in Definition 4.1.1. Examples are given by graphs (Example 4.1.1), where this second coproduct is given by contraction of connected subgraphs, hypergraphs (Example 4.1.2) with a similar construction, and posets (Example 4.1.3). A CTDB is always commutative (Proposition 4.1.9), but not necessarily cocommutative, as it is shown by the CTDB of posets P.To any element b P BrXs of a CTDB B is associated a set of equivalences Erbs Ď ErXs (Notation 4.1.1). We show in Proposition 4.1.7 that Erbs has always a smaller element „pmq 

> b

(which is always the equality of X) and a greater element „pM q 

> b

, which is a refinement of the equivalence „pic q 

> b

,whose classes are the indecomposable components of b. The most interesting cases of CTDBs are when „pM q 

> b

“„ pic q 

> b

for any b P B: they are called regular. We give eight characterizations of regular CTDBs in Proposition 4.1.15; one of the most useful is that the counit of the second coproduct 

δ is the character ϵB 

> `

of the third chapter. Graphs, hypergraphs, posets, and more generally any non-degenerate CTDB is regular (Proposition 4.1.16). A non regular CTDB has, in some sense, a defect of gradation, and we explain how to correct this, and to reduce ourselves to the case of regular CTDBs. Regular CTDBs are closely related to operads: we show in Theorem 4.1.22 that their subspecies of indecomposable elements are given an operadic structure, inducing the second coproduct δ. If B is a regular CTDB, then its fundamental polynomial invariant, as defined in Theorem 1.3.6, is ϕB

> `

. The double structure allows to have an expression for the coefficients of this polynomial invariant with the help of a character gB 

> ``

(Theorem 4.1.24). We introduce in the second section the weaker notion of quasi-cocommutative CTBs. The definition of these objects is rather technical (see Definition 4.2.1) and we won’t detail it now. They generalize non-degenerate twisted double bialgebras, and several results on the latter can be extended to them, as we shall see. Generally, a quasi-cocommutative CTB B does not have a second coproduct δ, but a coaction-like map ξ can be defined from B to Exch ` b B. First of all, a set of equivalences Erbs is associated to any element b P B (Definition 4.2.5), and an element 

C`pb, „q P Exch ` is associated to any „P Erbs (Theorem 4.2.10). In the case of a CTDB, 

C`pb, „q “ C`pb{„q ; however, when B is only quasi-cocommutative, b{„ is not necessarily well-defined. This map ξ is sufficient to find a cancellation-free formula for the antipode and the eulerian idempotent (Corollary 4.2.13). The fundamental polynomial invariant is also generalized to the level of quasi-cocommutative twisted combinatorial bialgebras (Proposition 4.2.14), and it is proved to be compatible with ξ. A formula for the coefficients of this polynomial invariant can be found in Theorem 4.2.17. These results will be used in the forthcoming chapter 6, on a twisted bialgebra on trees introduced in [78]. We also prove that any commutative twisted combinatorial bialgebra B contains a maximal quasi-cocommutative combinatorial twisted subbialgebra Bqcom ,and that this construction is functorial (Theorem 4.2.18 and Proposition 4.2.19). In the third section of this chapter, we turn back to Exch ` and look for a second coproduct 

δ on it, hoping to make it a CTDB. As it is not quasi-cocommutative, the results implies that 109 this does not exist, and we have to restrict ourselves, at least to Exch qcom  

> `

. In fact, this is not enough, and we prove in Theorem 4.3.1 the existence of a twisted combinatorial twisted subbialgebra Exch double  

> `

, such that: • Exch double  

> `

can be given a unique coproduct δ, making it double. • If B is a twisted combinatorial twisted subbialgebra of Exch ` which can be given a co-product δ1, making it a CTDB, then B Ď Exch double  

> `

and δ1 “ δB.A consequence of this result is that if B is a CTB such that ΓB 

> `

is injective, then B has at most one coproduct δ making it double (Corollary 4.3.2). This includes the CTDB G of graphs and 

P of posets. Unfortunately, in general, if B is a CTDB, then ΓB 

> `

does not induce a morphism of CTDBs from B to Exch double  

> `

, and we give a necessary and sufficient condition for ΓB 

> `

to be so in Theorem 4.3.5, when B is non-degenerate – note that in this case, the only potential morphism of CTDBs from B to Exch double  

> `

is ΓB

> `

. This condition is about the conservation of indecomposable components, and is always satisfied when ΓB 

> `

is injective (Corollary 4.3.6). For the case of a quasi-cocommutative CTB B, we can ask ourselves is the map ξ is a left coaction from Exch double  

> `

onto B. This is not the case in general, and we give a necessary and sufficient condition for this in Proposition 4.3.9. 

# 4.1 Combinatorial twisted double bialgebras 

4.1.1 Definition and examples

# 4.2 Quasi-cocommutative CTBs 

We now introduce quasi-cocommutative CTBs. The definition is rather technical, but its interest will appear in the next section, when we will try to introduce a structure of CTDB on Exch `.

4.2.1 Definition and examples 

Definition 4.2.1. Let B be a CTB. We shall say that it is quasi-cocommutative if it is commuta-tive and if for any finite set X, any b P BrXs, and any pX1, . . . , X kq and any pXσp1qq, . . . , X σpkqq P 

C´pbq with σ P Sk such that b|iXi and b|iXσpiq are indecomposable for any i P r ks, then 

@i P r ks, b|iXi “ b|σ´1piqXi .

Remark 4.2.1 . If B is a cocommutative CTB, then it is quasi-cocommutative. Therefore, G,

pHG , m, ∆Ă,Ă, ∆X,Xq are quasi-cocommutative. 

Example 4.2.1 . The CTBs P and QP are quasi-cocommutative, but not cocommutative, as the coproduct is given by restriction. 

Example 4.2.2 . 1. The CTB pHH , m, ∆X,Ăq is not quasi-cocommutative. Let G be the hy-pergraph on r4s whose hyperedges are given by 

HE pGq “ tt 1u, t2u, t3u, t4u, t1, 2u, t1, 3u, t1, 2, 3, 4uu .

HE pG|Y q “ tt 4uu , HE pG|XY q “ tt 4uu .

Hence, G|X , G|XX , G|Y , and G|XY are connected, so indecomposable. Moreover,

# Internal products and sub-objects 

# Introduction 

We introduce in this chapter more exotic structures on CTBs, using the Hadamard product of species. If P and Q are two species, the Hadamard product of P and Q is given on any finite set X by 

P b QrXs “ PrXs ˆ QrXs.

The Hadamard product of two CTBs is also a CTB (Proposition 5.1.2), but this is not true for CTDBs. An internal product on a CTB B is defined as an associative morphism of CTBs from B b B to B (Definition 5.1.4). For example, Exch and its sub-CTBs Exch ` and Exch ´

have an internal product, given by the intersection (Proposition 5.1.5); the CTBs of graphs, of hypergraphs, and of oriented graphs have also internal products, given by the union of set of hyperedges or arcs (Propositions 5.1.6 and 5.1.7). Another example is given by quasi-posets, with the transitive closure (Proposition 5.1.8). A major difference of this notion with the preceding ones is that it is not compatible with the bosonic Fock functor. Another application of the Hadamard product is the notion of coloration of a CTB (Definition 5.2.1): if Ω is a set and B is a CTB, we can define another CTB FΩrBs “ B b DΩ, whose objects are the objects of B, given an extra coloration by elements of Ω (Proposition 5.2.2). If B is a CTDB and Ω is a commutative semigroup, then FΩrBs is a CTDB (Proposition 5.2.5). An example is given by quasi-posets, which can be obtained from posets by a coloration by elements of Ną0. This formalism is used to study the formal flow associated to a CTB B. A similar task has been done in [7] for free Novikov algebras (objects which can also be lifted to the level of species, but not as a CTB) and for posets. We decorate B with elements ω1, . . . , ω k of a set Ω,and define in this way k infinitesimal characters ιi of B. We then define a character of FΩrBs by 

fΩ “ exp pι1q ˚ ¨ ¨ ¨ ˚ exp pιkq,

and the formal flow is the polynomial invariant of FΩrBs associated to fΩ, that is to say F B 

> Ω

“

ϕFΩrBs 

> fΩ

(Definition 5.2.8). As a consequence, the infinitesimal generator of F B 

> Ω

is 

dF B

> Ω

dT p0q “ ln pexp pι1q ˚ ¨ ¨ ¨ ˚ exp pιkqq ,

which shows the strong link between this construction and the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula in the group of characters of B. We give a combinatorial interpretation of the values of the formal flows when evaluated to an element of N, leading to the notion of B-partitions associated to a decorated element of B (Theorem 5.2.9). In the case of posets, we recover the classical notion of P-partitions [60], see Example 5.2.3. We finally prove that the formal flow is compatible with 

ΓB

> ´

, and with ΓB 

> `

when B is non-degenerate (Corollary 5.2.12). The second section is devoted to the definition of sub-objects of a CTB. The first one is the greatest cocommutative sub-CTB cocom B of a given CTB B, whose existence is proved in 150 Proposition 5.3.2. This can be rather small: for example, for the CTB P of posets, we only obtain the discrete elements. It is more interesting for Exch , and we give a description of cocom Exch 

with the help of an invariance condition under the action of symmetric groups (Definition 5.3.4), which leads to a description of cocom Exch in terms of sets of equivalences. The two other sub-objects are inspired by remarkable properties of famous families of polynomials. The first example is given by the reciprocity principle for Ehrhart polynomials: it is well-known that the Ehrhart polynomial Ehr pP q and the strict Ehrhart polynomial Ehr spP q of a poset P on a set of cardinality n (and more generally, of certain polytopes of dimension n) are related by the formula 

Ehr spP qp´ T q “ p´ 1qnEhr pP qp T q.

In other words, for the CTB of posets P, for any finite set X,

@P P PrXs, ϕP

> `

pP qp´ T q “ p´ 1q|X|ϕP

> ´

pP q.

We shall say that P satisfies the reciprocity principle (Definition 5.3.7). We prove that any CTB 

B has a sub-CTB Brecip satisfying the reciprocity principle, maximal for the inclusion, and that this construction is functorial (Proposition 5.3.10). As a consequence, the sub-CTB Exch recip 

> `

gives all the polynomial invariants with the reciprocity principle, and we show that there are more such examples than the Ehrhart polynomials of posets, see Examples 5.3.2 and 5.3.3. The second example is given by the alternating sign condition, satisfied by chromatic polynomials of graphs (Definition 5.3.12). We prove that any CTB B has a sub-CTB Balt satisfying the alternating sign condition, maximal for the inclusion, and that this construction is functorial (Proposition 5.3.13). This condition will be explored with more details in the cocommutative case in the forthcoming chapter 7.

5.2.2 Formal flow and B-partitions 

Definition 5.2.6. Let B be a CTB. Let X be a finite set, of cardinality denoted by n, b P BrXs,

f : X ÝÑ r ns be a bijection, and g : X ÝÑ r N s be a map. We define a relation on X as the lexicographic order associated to the pair pg, f q:

@x, y P X, x ďg,f y if gpxq ă gpyq or pgpxq “ gpyq and f pxq ď f pyqq .

As f is a bijection, ďg,f is a total order on X. We then write X “ t x1 ďg,f ¨ ¨ ¨ ď g,f xnu. We shall say that g is a B-partition of pb, f q if pt x1u, . . . , txnuq P C``pbq.

159 Example 5.2.3 . Let us consider the CTB of posets P. Let X be a finite set, and P P PrXs. If 

X “ t x1, . . . , x nu, then pt x1u, . . . , txnuq P C``pP q if, and only if, 

@i, j P r ns, xi ďP xj ùñ i ď j. 

Let f : X ÝÑ r ns a bijection and g : X ÝÑ r N s be a map. Reformulating Definition 5.2.6, g is a P-partition of pP, f q, if, and only if 

@x, y P X, x ďP y ùñ gpxq ă gpyq

or gpxq “ gpyq and f pxq ď f pyq.

This is equivalent to 

@x, y P X, x ďP y ùñ gpxq ď gpyq,

pf pxq ą f pyq and x ďP yq ùñ gpxq ă gpyq.

We recover the notion of P-partitions of an indexed poset [60, 40].

5.3.1 A lemma on twisted subcoalgebras

# An example based on rooted forests 

# Introduction 

We study in this chapter a CTB on rooted trees. The most famous Hopf algebra of rooted trees is perhaps the one due to Connes and Kreimer [19, 13, 28], related to Butcher’s group of Runge-Kutta’s methods, whose construction can be easily led to the level of CTBs. For example, the Butcher-Connes-Kreimer coproduct gives 

∆BCK ´ ¯

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` 3 b ` 3 b ` b ,

∆BCK 

ˆ ˙

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` b ` b ` b ` b ` b ,

∆BCK 

ˆ ˙

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` 2 b ` b ` b ,

∆BCK 

˜ ¸ 

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` b ` b ` b .

This can be seen as a sub-CTB of P, when one considers rooted forests as Hasse graphs of posets, as in Definition 6.1.1. We consider here a variant, defined in [14] and studied in [78] in the context of the use of Hopf algebras for linguistics [64], where the coproduct is given by 

∆L ´ ¯

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` 3 b ` 3 b ,

∆L

ˆ ˙

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` b ` b ,

∆L

ˆ ˙

“ 1 b ` b 1 ` 2 b ,

∆L

We show in Theorem 6.3.1 that the application of the bosonic Fock functor to both RF and 

RF ℓ gives the same Hopf algebra on rooted trees, with the coproduct ∆ (Theorem 6.3.1), with quotient Xiao’s Hopf algebra of reduced rooted forests. The quasi-cocommutativity of RF allows to find an easy cancellation-free formula for the antipode of FrRF s “ FrRF ℓs. Grouping the terms according the terms of this formula according to the reduction, we obtain a formula for Xiao’s Hopf algebra of reduced forests, with a combinatorial interpretation of the coefficients in terms of partitions of the vertices (Proposition 6.3.4), answering a question of [78].

Proposition 6.3.2. Let F be a reduced rooted forest, and I be a nonempty subset of LpF q. We shall say that I if tree-compatible if all the elements of I belong to the same tree of F . If so, there exists a unique vI P V pF q, such that for any v P V pF q, any element of I is a descendant of v if, and only if, v ďF vI . such that there exists y P I, with vI ďF v ďF y, and all the edges between these vertices. 

185 Proof. Let us denote by VI pF q the set of vertices v of F such that any element of I is a descendant of v. Then x ďF v and y ďF v, by definition of VI pF q. As F is a rooted forest, x and y are comparable for ďF . Therefore, pVI pF q, ďF q is totally ordered, so as a minimal element.

# The non-degenerate and cocommutative case 

# Introduction 

We now restrict ourselves to non-degenerate cocommutative CTBs, and show how the preceding results simplify. For example, if B is a cocommutative CTB, the polynomials invariants ϕB 

> ˘

take their values in ZrT s and are unitary (and ϕB 

> ´

is in fact reduced to a single unitary monomial). We prove the converse when B is double (Theorem 7.1.2). A major simplification is the existence of a (partial) restriction map (Proposition 7.1.1). Another one is the replacement of Exch ` by several CTBs of hypergraphs, as we shall see. Firstly, if B is a non-degenerate cocommutative CTB, we show how to associate, to any convenient subspecies of B, a morphism of CTBs from B to the CTB 

HG of hypergraphs (Theorem 7.2.1), and show that any morphism of CTBs from B to Exch `

can be written as the composition of such a morphism with ΓHG  

> `

(Corollary 7.2.2). Secondly, we use this result with two particular convenient subspecies of B, the subspecies of indecomposable elements (giving the morphism θB

> CS

), and the subspecies of minimal indecomposable elements (giving the morphism θB

> AC

). Let us detail these two morphisms. If b P BrXs, θB

> CS

pbq is the hypergraph on X whose hyperedges are the subsets Y Ď X such that b|Y indecomposable. Such a hypergraph is a connectedness system (Definition 7.2.5): • If Y Ď X is of cardinality one, then Y is a hyperedge of θB

> CS

pbq.• If Y and Z are two intersecting hyperedges of θB

> CS

pbq, then Y Y Z is also a hyperedge of 

θB

> CS

pbq.Connectedness systems define a sub-CTDB of HG denoted by CS (Theorem 7.2.7). We prove that 

θBCS is the unique morphism of CTBs from B to CS that conserves the number of indecomposable components; if B is double, then it is the unique morphism of CTDBs from B to CS (Theorem 7.2.8). As a consequence, all the fundamental polynomial invariants of cocommutative CTDBs are in fact chromatic polynomials of connectedness systems. Unfortunately, if B is a CTDB, the morphism ΓB 

> `

from B to Exch ` does not always induce a morphism of CTDBs from B to 

Exch double  

> `

, and we prove the existence of a sub-CTDB Bdouble of B, maximal for the inclusion, such that ΓBdouble  

> `

does, and that this construction is functorial (Propositions 7.2.12 and 7.2.13). If b P BrXs, θB

> AC

pbq is the hypergraph on X whose hyperedges are the subsets Y Ď X with 

b|Y indecomposable, and minimal for this property. Such a hypergraph is called an antichain cover (Definition 7.2.14): • Any hyperedge of θB

> AC

pbq is of cardinality ě 2.• If Y Ď Z are two hyperedges of θB

> AC

pbq, then Y “ Z.188 This defines a sub-CTB AC of HG (Theorem 7.2.15), unfortunately not a sub-CTDB. We prove that ΓAC  

> `

is injective, and that AC is a maximal subspecies of HG for this property (Theorem 7.2.19). This implies that ΓAC  

> `

pAC q is the greatest cocommutative sub-CTB of Exch ` (Corol-lary 7.2.20), and that any morphism from a cocommutative CTB to Exch ` takes its values in 

ΓAC  

> `

pAC q. As we mentioned before, AC is not a sub-CTDB of HG . We prove the existence of a sub-CTB AC double , maximal for the inclusion, which can be made double, and that this double structure is unique (Proposition 7.2.24). Moreover, ΓAC double  

> `

is a morphism of CTDBs from 

AC double to Exch double  

> `

.We finally show how the fundamental polynomial invariant, composed with θBCS , can be used to give a formula for the antipode of B, even if B is not double (Proposition 7.2.29). The chapter ends with a result on the alternating sign condition in the non-degenerate and cocommutative condition (Theorem 7.2.30). This implies that all the fundamental polynomial invariants from a cocommutative CTB with the alternating sign condition are in fact the ones with no "hole" in their coefficients, and are equal to the chromatic polynomial of some graph.

Let us give the definition of a connectedness system [73, Entry A072446]. 

Definition 7.2.5. Let G be a hypergraph. We shall say that G is a connectedness system if: 1. For any x P V pGq, txu P HE pGq.2. If A, B P HE pGq and A X B ‰ H , then A Y B P HE pGq.The subspecies of HG of connectedness systems is denoted by CS .Remark 7.2.3 . A connected connectedness system is called a clutter [77]. The following array gives the cardinality of CS rXs and HG rXs according to the cardinality of |X|. We denote by 

HG `rXs the set of hypergraphs on X which contains all the singletons txu, for x P X.

Lemma 7.2.6. Let G be a hypergraph on a finite set X. The following assertions are equivalent: 1. G is a connectedness system. 2. For any Y Ď X, G|Y is connected if, and only if, Y P HE pGq.Proof. 2. ùñ 1. For any x P X, G|t xu is connected, so txu P HE pGq. Let A, B P HE pGq, such that A X B ‰ H . Then G|A and G|B are connected, as A and B are hyperedges of G. As 

A X B ‰ H , G|AYB is connected. Therefore, A Y B P HE pGq.

1. ùñ 2. Let Y Ď X. Obviously, if Y P HE pGq, then G|Y is connected. Let us assume that 

G|Y is connected. As the singletons of X are hyperedges of X, Y contains hyperedges of G. Let us consider a hyperedge Z Ď Y of G, maximal for the inclusion. Let us assume that Z Ĺ Y . As 

G|Y is connected, there exists Z1 P HE pGq, such that H Ĺ Z X Z1 Ĺ Z and Z1 Ď Y . Therefore, as G is a connectedness system, Z Ĺ Z Y Z1 Ď Y , and Z Y Z1 P HE pGq. This contradicts the maximality of Z. So Z “ Y P HE pGq.

Remark 7.2.15 . As a consequence, if B is a cocommutative CTB with the alternating signs condition (or even with the seemingly weaker condition 2 of Theorem 7.2.30), the non-zero polynomials ϕB

# Structure of Exch , Exch ˘ and Exch `` 

# Introduction 

This last chapter does not consider anymore the combinatorial structure of Exch and its sub-objects, but aims to understand their bialgebraic underlying structures. The main tool is the generalization of Loday’s rigidity theorem for infinitesimal bialgebras [58], which we rise to the level of species. We introduce twisted infinitesimal bialgebras (Definition 8.1.3). The main exam-ples are free twisted algebras, with the concatenation product and deconcatenation coproduct. They are in fact the unique examples (under a condition of connectedness), by the rigidity The-orem 8.1.5. We shall use this Theorem to show that Exch , Exch `, Exch ´ and Exch `` are all cofree twisted coalgebras, with the introduction of complementary products. The problem of the product is solved by Theorem 8.1.9, which states that if B is a commu-tative cofree twisted bialgebra, then it is isomorphic to a shuffle twisted bialgebra. The proof of this Theorem is widely based on the eulerian idempotent and its properties. The introduction of a new product ˛ on Exch , Exch ` and Exch ``, and ˛ on Exch ´ then proves that they are isomorphic to shuffle twisted CTBs (Theorems 8.1.11 and 8.1.14), in a nontrivial way. 

# 8.1 Twisted infinitesimal bialgebras 

8.1.1 Definition and rigidity theorem 

Let us recall this definition, due to [58], and the main results on these objects. 

Definition 8.1.1. An infinitesimal bialgebra is a triple pA, m, ∆q, such that: 1. pA, m q is a unitary and associative algebra. 2. pA, ∆q is a counitary and coassociative coalgebra. 3. For any a, b P A, ∆pab q “ p a b 1q∆pbq ` ∆paqp 1 b bq ´ a b b.Example 8.1.1 . Let V be a vector space. We equip the tensor algebra T pV q with the concate-nation product mconc and the deconcatenation coproduct ∆conc . Then pA, m conc , ∆conc q is an infinitesimal bialgebra. 

Theorem 8.1.2. [58] Let pA, m, ∆q be an infinitesimal bialgebra, connected as a coalgebra. Then 

pA, m, ∆q is isomorphic to pT pPrim pAqq , m conc , ∆conc q.

Let us give an infinitesimal version of these results. 

Definition 8.1.3. A twisted infinitesimal bialgebra is a triple pQ, m, ∆q such that: 1. pQ, m q is a unitary and associative twisted algebra.

pΦ b Φq ˝ ∆X1,X 2 pxq “ ∆X1,X 2 ˝ Φpxq “ 0.

By minimality of |X|, Φ is injective on T pPrim pQqqr X1s and T pPrim pQqqr X2s. Therefore, 

∆X1,X 2 pxq “ 0, so x P Prim pT pPrim pQqqqr Xs “ Prim pQqr Xs. As a consequence, Φpxq “ x “ 0:this is a contradiction. So Φ is injective. 214 8.1.2 Cocommutative products on a cofree twisted coalgebra
